# My Portfolio Website

## You
*23 Sept 2026, 5:31*
I want to create my portfolio website, and I don't have much idea about how to create a website and how to, like, host it, and what will be the storage and what animation can be used and how it's done. I just need to make use of AI and make it, like, a good, clean, and eye-pleasing, and filled with, like, good things and also animations that can be seen on mobiles too. So I just want to make a good website, but how can I do it? Can you let me know, and what are the things that can be included in it? Plus let me know in detail, because I don't want to get confused.

## ChatGPT
*23 Sept 2026, 5:31 · gpt-5-6*
Absolutely. Since you’re starting from **“I don’t really know how websites are built/hosted”**, I’d avoid throwing you into HTML/CSS/React tutorials first.

The better approach is: **decide what your portfolio should communicate → let AI help build it → understand the important pieces → deploy it → gradually learn how it works.**

And because your goal is cybersecurity, your portfolio can actually become a **project itself** that demonstrates your skills.

## 1. What we should build

I’d recommend a **modern, single-page cybersecurity portfolio** first.

Think of it like:

```text
┌─────────────────────────────────────┐
│ NAVBAR                              │
│ Arhaan     About Skills Projects... │
├─────────────────────────────────────┤
│                                     │
│       CYBERSECURITY / IT            │
│       YOUR HERO SECTION             │
│                                     │
│   Short introduction                │
│   [View Projects] [Contact Me]      │
│                                     │
│        ↓ scroll animation            │
├─────────────────────────────────────┤
│ ABOUT ME                            │
├─────────────────────────────────────┤
│ SKILLS                              │
│ Linux | Networking | Security | ... │
├─────────────────────────────────────┤
│ PROJECTS                            │
│                                     │
│ [Project] [Project] [Project]       │
├─────────────────────────────────────┤
│ EXPERIENCE                          │
│                                     │
│ VAPT Internship                    │
│ Training / Certifications           │
├─────────────────────────────────────┤
│ CERTIFICATIONS                      │
├─────────────────────────────────────┤
│ LEARNING / CURRENTLY BUILDING       │
├─────────────────────────────────────┤
│ GITHUB / LINKEDIN / RESUME          │
├─────────────────────────────────────┤
│ CONTACT                             │
├─────────────────────────────────────┤
│ FOOTER                              │
└─────────────────────────────────────┘
```

The important thing is **not to cram everything onto the homepage**.

Your portfolio should make someone think:

> “This person is actively learning cybersecurity, actually builds things, documents their work, and is serious about entering the field.”

---

# 2. What should be included?

I'd divide it into these sections.

### ① Hero

This is the first thing someone sees.

Something like:

> **Hi, I'm Arhaan.**  
> Cybersecurity Enthusiast | MCA Student | VAPT Learner

Then a short sentence:

> I’m building my skills in cybersecurity, Linux, networking and web application security through hands-on projects and continuous learning.

Buttons:

**View My Projects**  
**Download Resume**

And small links:

GitHub · LinkedIn · Email

---

# 3. About Me

Don't write your entire life story.

Something like:

> I'm an MCA student building my career in cybersecurity. I'm currently focused on Linux, networking, web application security and vulnerability assessment.
>
> I believe cybersecurity is best learned by doing, so I document my learning, build practical projects and continuously work on improving my technical skills.

Then perhaps:

```text
🎓 MCA Student
🔐 Cybersecurity
🐧 Linux
🌐 Networking
🛡️ VAPT
💻 Web Security
```

---

# 4. Skills

Don't just make a giant list of 50 technologies.

Group them.

### Cybersecurity

- VAPT
- Web Application Security
- OWASP Top 10
- Vulnerability Assessment
- Security Testing

### Operating Systems

- Linux
- Windows

### Networking

- TCP/IP
- DNS
- HTTP/HTTPS
- DHCP
- SSH
- Networking fundamentals

### Tools

- Burp Suite
- Nmap
- Wireshark
- Metasploit
- Git/GitHub
- VirtualBox

### Programming / Scripting

- Python
- Bash
- HTML
- CSS
- JavaScript

And importantly:

**Only list things you can actually explain in an interview.**

---

# 5. Projects - this is probably the most important section

Instead of:

> "I know Nmap."

Show:

> **Network Reconnaissance Lab**

Then:

```text
What I built
What I learned
Tools used
What I discovered
GitHub
```

For example:

### 🔎 Network Reconnaissance Lab

> Practiced host discovery, port scanning and service enumeration in a controlled lab environment.

**Tools:** Nmap · Linux

**[View Project]**

---

Another:

### 🛡️ Web Application Security Lab

> Practiced identifying common web vulnerabilities using intentionally vulnerable applications.

**Tools:** Burp Suite · Linux

**[View Project]**

---

And your internship can eventually become:

### 🚨 VAPT Internship Journey

> Documented my preparation and hands-on learning journey in vulnerability assessment and penetration testing.

**[Read Case Study]**

This is much stronger than simply saying:

> "VAPT Intern"

You can have a timeline.

```text
2026
│
├── VAPT Intern
│   Aerotrace Forensics
│
├── Cybersecurity Training
│
└── MCA
```

Clicking an experience could open more information.

Keep this clean.

For example:

```text
Google Cybersecurity Professional Certificate
Coursera

[View Certificate]
```

Don't make the certificates section enormous.

# 8. "Currently Learning"

This could make your portfolio feel much more alive.

For example:

### Currently Learning

```text
████████░░  Linux
███████░░░  Networking
██████░░░░  Web Security
```

```text
Currently learning
Networking fundamentals

Practicing
```

# 9. GitHub activity

This can be really nice.

You could have:

> **Building in Public**

and show:

```text
GitHub
──────────────
Recent projects
Repositories
```

We can eventually connect GitHub so the website automatically displays your repositories.

That means you don't have to manually update the website every time you create a project.

# 10. Resume

Have a clear button:

**Download Resume**

The PDF can open/download separately.

---

# 11. Contact

Keep this simple.

```text
Let's connect.

Email
LinkedIn
GitHub

[Email Me]
```

You don't necessarily need a complicated contact form initially.

## ChatGPT
*23 Sept 2026, 5:35 · gpt-5-6*
Yes - and the **admin panel question is important**. For the kind of portfolio you want, I would actually design the system with one from the beginning, but keep it **small and private**, rather than building a huge CMS.

The easiest way to understand the whole thing is to separate it into **5 parts**:

```text
                 YOUR PORTFOLIO
                       │
        ┌──────────────┴──────────────┐
        │                             │
     WEBSITE                       ADMIN
   What visitors see             Only you see
        │                             │
        └──────────────┬──────────────┘
                       │
                    DATABASE
                Your portfolio data
                       │
                  HOSTING / SERVER
                       │
                    DOMAIN
              yourname.com / .in
```

## 1. What I recommend building

For you, I'd build a **premium cybersecurity portfolio**, not the stereotypical:

> black background + green Matrix rain + hacker image + skull + terminal everywhere

That can very easily look like a cheap template.

Instead, I'd go for:

### **Dark cinematic + technical + minimal**

Think:

- deep charcoal/black background
- off-white typography
- one controlled accent colour
- subtle blue/cyan/green security-inspired highlights
- thin borders
- glass/blur used sparingly
- technical grid patterns
- subtle animated particles
- smooth section transitions
- elegant cards
- occasional terminal/code visualizations
- lots of whitespace

The cybersecurity identity should be **felt**, rather than screaming "HACKER" everywhere.

---

# 2. The technology I'd use

You don't need to learn everything before starting.

I'd build it with something like:

```text
Frontend
   ↓
Next.js
React
TypeScript
Tailwind CSS

Animations
   ↓
Framer Motion / Motion
CSS animations
Optional Three.js/WebGL

Backend
   ↓
Next.js API/server functions

Database
   ↓
Supabase / PostgreSQL

Admin
   ↓
Custom private dashboard

Hosting
   ↓
Vercel

Domain
   ↓
Your own .com / .in domain
```

And AI can help us write a **large portion of the code**.

You don't need to become a professional React developer before beginning.

But I **do** want you to understand what we're building, rather than blindly copying AI-generated code.

---

# 3. Do you need a database?

### Initially: technically, no.

A simple portfolio could store everything inside code:

```text
projects.ts
skills.ts
experience.ts
```

But then imagine you finish a new project.

You'd have to:

```text
Open code
↓
Find project section
↓
Change code
↓
Commit
↓
Deploy
```

That's annoying.

### So for YOUR version:

I'd use a database.

Something like:

```text
Portfolio Database
│
├── Profile
├── Projects
├── Skills
├── Experience
├── Certifications
├── Education
├── Social Links
└── Site Settings
```

Then your website retrieves that information automatically.

---

# 4. And yes - an Admin Panel

This is what I'd recommend.

You visit:

```text
yourdomain.com/admin
```

You log in.

And see:

```text
┌──────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Portfolio Admin                      │
├──────────────────────────────────────┤
│                                      │
│ Dashboard                            │
│                                      │
│ 👤 Profile                           │
│ 💼 Experience                        │
│ 🛠 Skills                            │
│ 🚀 Projects                          │
│ 🎓 Certifications                    │
│ 📚 Education                         │
│ 🔗 Social Links                      │
│ ⚙️ Site Settings                     │
│                                      │
└──────────────────────────────────────┘
```

Then you can click:

**Projects → Add Project**

and get:

```text
Project Title
[_____________________]

Short Description
[_____________________]

Long Description
[_____________________]

Technologies
[ Linux ] [ Nmap ] [ Burp Suite ]

GitHub URL
[_____________________]

Live Demo
[_____________________]

Project Image
[ Upload ]

Featured Project
☑

[ SAVE PROJECT ]
```

You save it.

The website automatically updates.

**No code editing required.**

---

```text
etc.
```

Not initially.

Your admin panel only needs to manage **your portfolio content**.

# 16. Animations

You specifically mentioned animations, and **yes, we can make the website feel very polished**.

But the rule should be:

> **Animation should improve the experience, not distract from the content.**

I'd use roughly these.

### On page load

Your hero text gently fades/slides in.

```text
ARHAAN
      ↓
Cybersecurity
      ↓
Description
      ↓
Buttons
```

---

### Scroll animations

As you scroll:

```text
About
   ↓
fade + slight movement

Skills
   ↓
cards appear

Projects
   ↓
cards reveal

Experience
   ↓
timeline progresses
```

---

### Hover effects

Project cards can slightly lift:

```text
Normal

┌──────────────┐
│ Project      │
└──────────────┘

Hover

      ↗
┌──────────────┐
│ Project      │
└──────────────┘
```

Very subtle.

---

### Background

Instead of Matrix rain, I'd consider:

- subtle grid
- moving gradient
- very faint particles
- network nodes
- animated noise texture
- radial glow

These can look much more premium.

---

# 17. What about a 3D animation?

We *can* use Three.js/WebGL.

For example, a subtle:

```text
        ●
       / \
      ●───●
       \ /
        ●
```

network structure floating behind your hero section.

But I wouldn't automatically add 3D just because we can.

3D can:

- increase loading time
- consume mobile battery
- cause performance problems
- look gimmicky if poorly designed

So we'd make it **progressive**:

```text
Desktop → richer animation

Tablet → reduced animation

Mobile → lightweight animation
```

---

# 18. Mobile is extremely important

We shouldn't make:

> Desktop website → squeeze it onto mobile.

Instead:

### Desktop

```text
Hero
──────────────────────
Text          Animation
──────────────────────

Projects
[ 1 ] [ 2 ] [ 3 ]
```

### Mobile

```text
Hero

Text

Animation

Buttons

↓

Projects

[ Project ]

[ Project ]

[ Project ]
```

And animations should automatically reduce where necessary.

We can also respect the user's:

**prefers-reduced-motion**

setting.

That means if someone has reduced motion enabled on their device, the website doesn't bombard them with animations.

---

# 19. Hosting

This sounds complicated but isn't.

Think:

```text
Your computer
      ↓
GitHub
      ↓
Vercel
      ↓
Internet
```

You build the website.

Push code to GitHub.

Vercel connects to the repository.

Whenever you update the website:

```text
GitHub
 ↓
Vercel automatically builds
 ↓
New version goes live
```

You don't need to manage your own physical server.

---

Eventually you could have something like:

```text
arhaan.dev
```

or

```text
arhaan.in
```

or another suitable domain.

Then:

```text
https://arhaan.dev
```

becomes your public portfolio.

# 21. Where does the database live?

If we use something like Supabase:

```text
                    INTERNET
                       │
                       ↓
                ┌─────────────┐
                │   VERCEL    │
                │   WEBSITE   │
                └──────┬──────┘
                       │
                       ↓
                ┌─────────────┐
                │  SUPABASE   │
                │             │
                │ PostgreSQL  │
                │ Auth        │
                │ Storage     │
                └─────────────┘
```

Supabase can handle things such as:

- database
- authentication
- file storage

So we don't need to build all of that from scratch.

---

# 22. What is "storage"?

There are actually two different things.

### Database

Stores things like:

```text
Project name
Description
Skills
GitHub URL
Experience
Certificate information
```

### File storage

Stores things like:

```text
Profile picture
Project screenshots
Certificate PDFs
Resume PDF
Project images
```

So:

```text
DATABASE
→ text/data

STORAGE
→ files/images/PDFs
```

That's an important distinction.

---

# 23. Do you need a backend?

For a simple static portfolio:

**No.**

But because you want:

- admin panel
- database
- editable projects
- editable experience
- image uploads
- authentication

then yes, we'll have a small backend/server layer.

You don't need to build a massive backend.

---

# 24. Security is especially important for YOUR website

This is where your portfolio can demonstrate that you're actually thinking like a cybersecurity person.

Your admin panel should have:

```text
/admin
   ↓
Authentication
   ↓
Protected dashboard
```

Not:

```text
/admin

Anyone can edit my portfolio 😂
```

We should also implement:

- secure authentication
- protected admin routes
- environment variables
- database access policies
- input validation
- secure file uploads
- HTTPS
- no exposed API keys
- no sensitive credentials in GitHub
- security headers
- rate limiting where appropriate

And later you can even have a small:

### Security

section showing:

> This portfolio is built with security and privacy in mind.

That is actually a nice touch for a cybersecurity portfolio.

---

First get the portfolio working.

# 26. What I would NOT put on it

Avoid things like:

❌ Matrix rain everywhere  
❌ Hacker mask images  
❌ "I am an ethical hacker 😈"  
❌ Fake skill percentages  
❌ 20 different animations  
❌ Giant spinning 3D globe  
❌ Random terminal windows everywhere  
❌ Excessive neon green  
❌ Fake "cyber attack" dashboards  
❌ Huge paragraphs  
❌ 30 technology logos  
❌ Fake statistics like "99% security expert"

Those are the things that can make a cybersecurity portfolio feel like a template.

# 28. How AI fits into this

This is the part I think will work very well for you.

You don't need to say:

> "AI, create my portfolio."

and accept whatever it produces.

Instead, we'll use AI as your **developer + designer + teacher**.

For example:

### Step 1

We decide the visual direction.

### Step 2

AI generates the initial UI.

### Step 3

We run it.

### Step 4

You look at it.

### Step 5

You tell me:

> "The hero feels too empty."

I'll help modify it.

Then:

> "The project cards look cheap."

We redesign them.

Then:

> "Mobile navigation isn't good."

We fix it.

Then:

> "I want the animation slower."

We adjust it.

So we're **iterating**, not blindly generating.

Since you said:

We can use **Replit's AI development environment** to help build the application from natural-language instructions, while still keeping the actual project/code under your control.

That means you can describe what you want and have AI help implement it, then we can inspect and improve the result.

You could also work locally with VS Code later when you're comfortable.

Things like:

   ↓

# 31. What your final system could look like

That's a **real, proper web application**, not just a static portfolio.

# 32. One important decision before we start

I would **not** start coding yet.

First, we should create the **complete blueprint/design specification** for your portfolio:

```text
01. Brand / identity
02. Colour palette
03. Typography
04. Homepage structure
05. Every section
06. Project-card design
07. Animations
08. Mobile behaviour
09. Admin dashboard
10. Database structure
11. Storage structure
12. Authentication
13. GitHub integration
14. Blog architecture
15. Hosting
16. Domain
17. Security
18. SEO
19. Performance
20. Deployment
```

Then we build it **step-by-step**, so you don't end up with 500 files generated by AI that you don't understand.

### My recommended final stack

| Part | Choice |
|---|---|
| Frontend | **Next.js + React + TypeScript** |
| Styling | **Tailwind CSS** |
| Animation | **Motion + CSS** |
| Advanced visual effects | **Three.js only where justified** |
| Database | **Supabase/PostgreSQL** |
| Authentication | **Supabase Auth** |
| File storage | **Supabase Storage** |
| Admin | **Custom private dashboard** |
| Code | **GitHub** |
| Hosting | **Vercel** |
| Domain | Your own `.in` / `.dev` / `.com` |
| AI development | **AI-assisted, iterative development** |

**And yes: I'd build the admin panel from the beginning**, but keep it intentionally simple. That way, six months from now, when you have 15 projects, 5 certifications and more experience, you won't have to ask a developer to change your website every time-you'll just log into `/admin`, update it, and publish.

If you want to proceed, the next step should be **Design Blueprint v1**: I'll map out exactly what your portfolio should look like section-by-section, including the cybersecurity visual style, animations, mobile behaviour, admin panel screens, and database fields **before we write any code**.

## ChatGPT
*24 Sept 2026, 15:34 · gpt-5-6*
No - **don't put the prompt in a `.txt` or `.md` file just to give it to Codex.** For what we're doing right now, paste the prompt directly into Codex's chat box.

Your developer was likely using `.md` files for a **different purpose**.

### The difference

**Prompt in Codex chat:**

```text
"Build X using these requirements..."
```

That's an instruction to the AI for a particular task.

**`.md` file:**

```text
AGENTS.md
README.md
PROJECT_PLAN.md
CHANGELOG.md
```

That's documentation/instructions that live **inside the project**.

For example, `AGENTS.md` can tell Codex:

```markdown
# Project Rules

- Use TypeScript.
- Use reusable components.
- Keep the site responsive.
- Don't install unnecessary dependencies.
- Follow accessibility best practices.
- Don't expose secrets.
```

Codex can then refer to those rules while working on the project.

### Why your developer asked for `.md`

Your developer probably wanted a **written specification/change document** so that:

- requirements don't get lost in chat
- another developer/AI can understand the project
- changes are documented
- you can keep a record of what was requested
- AI can read the project's instructions later

That's actually a **good practice for a larger project like this**.

---

## For our portfolio, we'll use both

I'll keep this simple:

### 1. Codex chat
For **actual tasks**:

> "Build the hero section."

> "Fix the mobile navbar."

> "Add the project cards."

### 2. `AGENTS.md`
For **permanent rules** that Codex should follow throughout the project.

### 3. `PROJECT_PLAN.md`
For our **overall portfolio blueprint**.

### 4. `CHANGELOG.md`
For important changes we make over time.

So eventually:

```text
Portfolio/
│
├── AGENTS.md          ← Rules for AI/developers
├── PROJECT_PLAN.md    ← What we're building
├── CHANGELOG.md       ← What changed
│
├── app/
├── components/
├── public/
└── ...
```

**But don't create these manually right now.** The prompt I gave you already tells Codex to create `AGENTS.md`.

For now, **paste that prompt directly into Codex chat**. That's the correct approach.

### 1. Small change → Codex chat only

Examples:

> "Connect Supabase."

Examples:

```text

DESIGN_SPEC.md
DATABASE.md
DEPLOYMENT.md
SECURITY.md
```

These become the **source of truth** for important parts of the project.

For example:

```text
DESIGN_SPEC.md
↓
How the website should look

DATABASE.md
↓
How our database is structured

SECURITY.md
↓
Security requirements

DEPLOYMENT.md
↓
How the website is deployed
```

## You
*24 Sept 2026, 17:00*
Quick clarification: Codex is OpenAI's product, not Anthropic's, so I don't have inside info on its limits - I'll just give general tips that apply to any AI coding assistant. For Claude, here's what actually helps.

**Why usage runs out fast (Claude and similar tools alike)**

Usage isn't just "number of messages" - it's tokens. Long conversations, big file uploads, tool use (web search, code execution), and heavy models all burn through your allowance faster than short, focused chats.

**How to make it last longer**

- **Start new chats for new topics.** Every message in a thread re-sends the whole conversation history, so a long thread costs more per message than a fresh one.
- **Edit instead of stacking follow-ups.** If a reply misses the mark, click edit on your original message and refine it, rather than sending "make it shorter," "now add X," etc. - each of those re-processes the entire thread.
- **Be specific up front.** Give full context and requirements in one message instead of trickling them in over several turns.
- **Batch related asks** into a single message rather than one question per message.
- **Watch attachments and tool use.** Large files and things like web search/code execution use more of your budget than plain text chat.
- **Use Projects if you're on Claude** - content there is cached and doesn't eat into your limit the same way repeated pasting would.
- **Pick a lighter model when you don't need the strongest one** - for simple tasks a smaller/faster model uses less of your allowance.

**What to do once it ends**

- Free/lower tiers reset on a timer (Claude's free tier resets daily; Pro resets roughly every 5 hours) - waiting is the no-cost option.
- If you're on a paid plan and need more right now, some plans let you buy extra usage instead of waiting (Claude Pro/Max, Team, Enterprise all have options for this - check your account's billing/usage settings).
- Switching to another platform temporarily is a completely reasonable stopgap - nothing wrong with using Codex, Claude, and others in parallel and just routing work to whichever has quota left at the moment.

If you want, tell me what plan you're on (free/Pro/Max/Team) and I can point you to the exact reset timing and paid-usage options for that tier.

Since your work is code that lives in files (unlike a chat that resets), switching agents is actually pretty manageable if you set it up right. Here's how to do it cleanly:

**1. Keep the project in version control (Git)**

This is the single biggest thing. Push to GitHub/GitLab. Any agent - Claude Code, Codex, Cursor, whatever - can then pull the exact current state instead of you having to explain "here's what exists so far."

**2. Maintain a living spec/context doc in the repo**

A `README.md` or `PROJECT_CONTEXT.md` that describes: what the project is, the stack, what's built, what's in progress, and what's next. You're already doing something close to this with PrintBloom's separate requirement files (attributes, invoice, rewards system, etc.) - that habit is exactly what makes agent-switching painless. Whichever agent you bring in next just reads those files first.

**3. Give the new agent a "handoff" prompt**

When you switch mid-task, don't just say "continue building the site." Say something like: *"This is an existing Next.js + Supabase project. Read README.md and the spec files in /docs. I was last working on [X]. Here's what's done and what's not."* Point it at the files rather than re-typing everything from memory.

**4. Keep environment/config portable**

`.env.example` (not the real secrets), a clear `package.json`, and setup instructions in the README mean a new agent - or a new tool session - can get the dev environment running without guesswork.

**5. Commit often, in small chunks**

If an agent's usage cuts out mid-task, a recent commit means you lose minutes, not hours. Treat every "usage limit reached" as a signal to commit what works before switching tools.

**6. Test before and after switching**

Different agents have different styles/conventions. After a handoff, run the app and check nothing broke or diverged from the existing patterns (naming, folder structure, etc.) before continuing.

The practical result: your actual state of truth is the **codebase + docs**, not any single agent's memory. Whoever/whatever you're talking to is replaceable as long as the repo and specs stay current.

That's exactly how I'd recommend we build your portfolio, especially because you're worried about Codex usage running out.

But I'd make a few changes for **your specific project**.

### What I would do for your portfolio

**1. GitHub - absolutely yes**

We should create your `my-portfolio` GitHub repo soon.

Your flow becomes:

```text
Codex
  ↓
Make changes
  ↓
Test
  ↓
Git commit
  ↓
GitHub
  ↓
Another agent can continue if needed
```

If Codex reaches its limit, your project doesn't disappear.

---

**2. Your existing `AGENTS.md` + `PROJECT_PLAN.md` are already doing part of this**

We don't need to suddenly create 10 documentation files.

Right now you already have:

```text
AGENTS.md
PROJECT_PLAN.md
```

And we have the Phase 3 specification.

Later, when we reach the backend stage, we can add something like:

```text
docs/
├── DATABASE.md
├── ADMIN_PANEL.md
└── DEPLOYMENT.md
```

**Only when they're actually needed.**

---

**3. `.env.example` - yes, but later**

When we introduce Supabase/Vercel/etc., we'll make sure secrets aren't committed.

For example:

```text
.env.local        ← secrets, NEVER commit
.env.example      ← variable names/placeholders, commit
```

That's important.

---

**4. Small Git commits - I strongly agree**

This is probably the biggest thing I'd add to our workflow.

Instead of:

> "Build my entire portfolio."

We'll do:

```text
Phase 3 UI
   ↓
commit

Visual refinement
   ↓
commit

Projects section
   ↓
commit

Supabase setup
   ↓
commit

Admin authentication
   ↓
commit
```

If an agent breaks something, we have a known-good checkpoint.

---

### One thing I DON'T recommend

Claude said:

> "Any agent can pull the exact current state."

That's true **if the GitHub repository is up to date**.

But don't assume every agent will automatically understand everything just because it has access to the repo.

For example, if Codex is halfway through a change and you immediately switch to another agent, the second agent may not know:

- what Codex was trying to accomplish
- which files it intended to change
- whether the implementation is incomplete
- why a particular architectural decision was made

So we should **finish or stop at clean checkpoints** whenever possible.

---

# Our safety net

I'd like our project workflow to become:

```text
             ┌───────────────┐
             │   GitHub repo │
             │  source truth │
             └───────┬───────┘
                     │
          ┌──────────┴──────────┐
          ↓                     ↓
       Codex                 Claude
          │                     │
          └──────────┬──────────┘
                     ↓
                 Your code
```

And the important files:

```text
Portfolio/
│
├── AGENTS.md
├── PROJECT_PLAN.md
├── README.md
├── package.json
├── src/
│   ├── app/
│   ├── components/
│   └── data/
│
└── docs/              ← add only when needed
```

So **you're not locked into Codex**.

If Codex lasts through the whole project, great.

If it doesn't, we can switch.

If Claude becomes useful for a particular task, we can use it.

If another free coding agent becomes better for a particular job, we can use that too.

### And importantly: you don't need to panic about the current limit.

You've already successfully built the foundation and Phase 3 UI. **We don't need to waste Codex usage on rebuilding anything.**

Our next step should be:

**1. Commit the current working Phase 3 → 2. visually upgrade the site → 3. commit again → 4. continue from there.**

That gives you a clean escape hatch at every major stage.

- visual refinements

### 3. VS Code + me - safest emergency fallback

This is actually important.

If every coding agent runs out, **your project doesn't stop**.

You can keep the project open in VS Code and tell me:

I can give you the exact file/change or a carefully scoped instruction for another tool. You can then run:

and commit the working result.

every few hours.

Before an agent makes a significant change:

```text
WORKING VERSION
      ↓
Git commit
      ↓
AI makes changes
      ↓
npm run lint
      ↓
npm run build
      ↓
YOU REVIEW WEBSITE
      ↓
Git commit
```

If an agent produces something ugly or breaks something:

**we don't accept it as the new baseline.**

We can revert to the previous commit.

So even if Codex suddenly runs out halfway through the project, we haven't lost control of the project.

A real-world brand toolkit also uses **Space Grotesk for headings and Inter for body text**, specifically because Space Grotesk provides personality while Inter improves readability for longer/smaller text. [precis.com](https://www.precis.com/brand-portal/visual-identity/typography)

This is the more **technical/engineering** option.

*24 Sept 2026, 20:00 · gpt-5-6*
Yes - **an `.md` spec is actually the better approach for this particular change**, especially because you're switching between agents and want the project to stay understandable.

And you're right about credits: **a longer prompt can consume more context/tokens**, but it's not simply “longer prompt = proportionally more credit.” The bigger cost is usually the agent reading files, reasoning, editing, running commands, and retrying. Clear instructions can actually **save credits by preventing wrong implementations and retries**. Current guidance from VS Code, GitHub, and Anthropic all emphasizes focused context, clear stopping conditions, and avoiding unnecessary context. [code.visualstudio.com](https://code.visualstudio.com/docs/agents/guides/context-engineering-guide) 

### What I'd do for your project

Instead of giving Gemini that huge prompt every time:

**Create one small file:**

```text
PHASE_4_HERO_INTERACTION.md
```

It becomes the **specification/source of truth** for this hero experiment.

Then your Gemini prompt becomes only:

```text
Read:
- AGENTS.md
- PROJECT_PLAN.md
- PHASE_4_HERO_INTERACTION.md

Implement Phase 4 exactly according to the specification.

Work ONLY on the hero section and its interactive visual.
Do not modify unrelated sections.

Before finishing:
- npm run lint
- npm run build
- fix all errors

Then summarize the files changed and validation results.
```

That's much cleaner.

### And importantly

We should **not make the MD enormous**.

The good approach is:

**MD = what we want + what we don't want + interaction behavior + visual direction + acceptance criteria**

**Agent = figures out how to implement it.**

That's better than telling it every tiny CSS/React decision. Anthropic specifically recommends the “right altitude”: specific enough to guide the agent, but not so rigid that the prompt becomes brittle. [anthropic.com](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/effective-context-engineering-for-ai-agents)

Also, because your previous hero experiment went wrong, I would make this spec include a **hard scope boundary**:

> If the implementation requires changing anything outside the hero component/styles, stop and reassess rather than redesigning the rest of the website.

That should help prevent another “MODETRACEEXPLORE” disaster. 😭

**So yes: let's make the MD.** It will also make this phase easy to hand off to Cursor/Claude/Codex later without relying on conversation history.

**Sources**

1. [Set up a context engineering flow in VS Code](https://code.visualstudio.com/docs/agents/guides/context-engineering-guide)
2. [Effective context engineering for AI agents \ Anthropic](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/effective-context-engineering-for-ai-agents)

Current State: Static presentation without interactive mode toggling, node inspection panels, or scroll-synchronized system telemetry.
2. Components/Files that Need Modification
src/app/page.tsx: Replace static hero markup with the client-rendered HeroSection orchestrator and attach section observer refs/attributes to main portfolio sections (#about, #skills, #projects, #experience, #certifications, #contact).
src/components/site-header.tsx: Add an active section / system state telemetry indicator synchronized with page scrolling.
src/app/globals.css: Add styles for Hero Core visual modes, inspect drawer/modal overlay, active node highlights, system telemetry badges, responsive mobile touch targets, and reduced-motion overrides.
src/data/portfolio.ts: Add data definitions for Hero Core operational modes (LEARN, TEST, BUILD), node inspection specs, and system telemetry metrics.
3. New Components/Files to Create
src/components/hero/hero-section.tsx: Client component orchestrating top-level Hero Core state (mode, inspect, system).
src/components/hero/hero-headline.tsx: Interactive stacked headline (LEARN / TEST / BUILD) linked to mode switching.
src/components/hero/hero-visual.tsx: Interactive system visual replacing the old network-map card visual. Displays interactive nodes, real-time data flows, and active mode highlights.
src/components/hero/inspect-panel.tsx: Accessible inspection panel/drawer displaying technical specifications, security logs, tools, and node metadata when an element is inspected.
src/components/hero/mode-selector.tsx: Control bar allowing manual mode switching between LEARN, TEST, and BUILD.
src/hooks/use-scroll-system.ts: Custom React hook using IntersectionObserver to track viewport scrolling, calculate progress, and synchronize SYSTEM state.
4. State Architecture
MODE State: 'LEARN' | 'TEST' | 'BUILD' (default: 'LEARN'). Controls active headline focus, available visual nodes, and security domain context.
INSPECT State: { isOpen: boolean; activeNodeId: string | null; nodeData: InspectNodeDetails | null }. Toggled on clicking interactive visual nodes or inspect triggers.
SYSTEM State: { activeSection: string; scrollProgress: number; status: 'NOMINAL' | 'ANALYZING' | 'SECURE'; telemetry: SystemTelemetry }. Automatically updated via scroll position and section intersection observers.
5. Animation Architecture
Transitions: Smooth CSS custom property and transform transitions with zero heavy external dependencies.
Frame Performance: Uses IntersectionObserver and requestAnimationFrame for scroll tracking to ensure 60fps performance without main-thread scroll jank.
Visual Motion: Subtle CSS keyframes for pulse and data-flow animations on visual nodes.
Reduced Motion: Under prefers-reduced-motion: reduce, all keyframes and smooth translations are disabled, providing instant, discrete state updates.
6. Interaction Between MODE, INSPECT, and SYSTEM
MODE: Dictates the active operational lens (LEARN = security analysis & fundamentals, TEST = VAPT & web app vulnerability assessment, BUILD = security architecture & lab implementation). Updating MODE highlights corresponding headline words, shifts visual node layouts, and alters data flow streams.
INSPECT: Provides deep-dive technical overlays into specific visual nodes within the active MODE or SYSTEM overview. Users can inspect security methodologies, tools used, logs, and technical metrics.
SYSTEM: Operates as the ambient environment controller. Tracks user movement down the portfolio page, updating system status indicators in the header and hero telemetry panel as sections enter the viewport.
7. SYSTEM Scroll Synchronization
Uses an IntersectionObserver listening to all major portfolio sections (#about, #skills, #projects, #experience, #certifications, #contact).
As a section crosses the viewport threshold, SYSTEM updates activeSection state, synchronizes active header navigation links, and triggers section reveal animations.
8. Mobile and Reduced-Motion Behavior
Mobile:
Clean responsive stacked layout (Hero Copy on top, Hero Visual below).
Inspect panel renders as a touch-friendly bottom drawer / full-width sheet with clear close triggers.
Generous touch targets (>44px) for all mode controls and node triggers.
Reduced-Motion:
Enforces prefers-reduced-motion: reduce across CSS and JS.
Disables ambient looping animations, continuous pulse effects, and sliding drawer transitions.
Replaces motion with immediate state changes and high-contrast indicators.
9. Risks & Mitigation
Hydration Mismatches: Ensure browser-only window/observer APIs execute inside useEffect with appropriate server fallback states.
Scroll Jank: Use non-blocking IntersectionObserver rather than raw window.onscroll listeners.
Accessibility: Manage focus trap and release (Escape key, focus restoration) when the Inspect panel is open, adhering to WAI-ARIA modal/drawer standards.
Note: No files have been modified yet. Please review and approve this plan to begin implementation.

## My recommendation: use OpenCode as your new main coding layer

**But not because OpenCode itself is "the smartest model."**

The important thing is that OpenCode is a **model/provider router + coding agent**. It supports 75+ providers and can also use local models. [opencode.ai](https://opencode.ai/docs/providers)

And here's the killer feature for your situation:

**OpenCode can use your existing GitHub Copilot account or ChatGPT Plus/Pro authentication.** Its official docs explicitly list both as supported subscription connections. [opencode.ai](https://opencode.ai/docs/providers)

So instead of thinking:

> "Which agent has 10% left?"

we should think:

> **"Which model/provider should OpenCode use for this particular task?"**

That's much more sustainable.

---

# Your new setup

I want you to settle on this:

| Tool | What YOU use it for |
|---|---|
| 🧠 **Me / ChatGPT** | Architecture, decisions, prompts, code review |
| 🛠️ **OpenCode** | **Primary coding agent** |
| 💻 **VS Code** | Editing, terminal, preview |
| 💾 **GitHub** | Permanent source of truth |

This is much cleaner.

---

# Why I'm choosing OpenCode

You just installed it, and it gives us something the other tools don't:

### It separates the agent from the model.

OpenCode supports providers including OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, GitHub Copilot, OpenRouter, NVIDIA, Ollama and many others. [opencode.ai](https://opencode.ai/docs/providers)

You can select the model with:

```text
/models
```

and OpenCode's docs explicitly recommend selecting from the available models rather than guessing model IDs. [opencode.ai](https://opencode.ai/v2/docs/models)

So later, if one provider's quota dies:

```text
OpenCode
   ↓
Provider A
   ❌ quota
   ↓
Provider B
   ↓
continue project
```

The **coding environment stays the same**.

That's exactly what you need.

---

# But there's one VERY important catch

Don't connect OpenCode to random "free API keys" just because they are free.

That's where free vibecoding can become messy.

OpenCode's own docs show that many providers require their own credentials/API keys. [opencode.ai](https://opencode.ai/docs/providers)

And OpenCode Zen itself isn't a magical unlimited free model pool; the official docs describe its console as pay-as-you-go, while OpenCode Go is a paid subscription. [opencode.ai](https://opencode.ai/v2/docs/console/models/)

So **we're not going to chase random free API providers.**

---

# First thing: connect your GitHub Copilot Free account

If you already have GitHub Copilot Free, this is worth trying.

GitHub's current Free plan provides:

- 2,000 code completions/month
- limited chat/agent usage
- Copilot CLI
- access to included models through auto-selection. [docs.github.com](https://docs.github.com/en/copilot/managing-copilot/managing-copilot-as-an-individual-subscriber/getting-started-with-copilot-on-your-personal-account/about-individual-copilot-plans-and-benefits?roistat_visit=2564200)

OpenCode can authenticate with GitHub Copilot. [opencode.ai](https://opencode.ai/docs/providers)

So inside OpenCode:

```text
/connect
```

Then look for:

```text
GitHub Copilot
```

Authenticate.

Then:

```text
/models
```

See what models are actually available to your account.

**Don't guess the model name.**

---

# What about your Antigravity 22%?

### Don't throw it away.

Antigravity's free Individual plan currently includes access to Gemini models, Claude Sonnet/Opus 4.6 and gpt-oss-120b, plus unlimited tab completions and command requests, with a basic weekly rate limit. [antigravity.google](https://antigravity.google/pricing)

So it's valuable.

But its agent quota is usage-based and refreshed weekly, and Google says the amount of work performed by the agent affects how quickly quota is consumed. [antigravity.google](https://antigravity.google/docs/plans?trk=public_post_comment-text)

Therefore:
